A unification of graded and substructural logics Volume: NN Journal: Electronic Notes in Theoretical Informatics and Computer Science Volume: NN This work was supported by NSF grant #2104535 “SHF: SMALL: Semantically and Practically Generalizing Graded Modal Types” Email: phanukaev@augusta.edu Email: harley.eades@pm.me
Peter Hanukaev phanukaev, Harley Eades III heades

Abstract
Type systems which account for resource sensitive computations can generally be split into two styles: First, substructural logics such as Linear Logic which seek to restrict weakening and contraction and reintroduce them in a controlled manner; And second, graded systems which allow weakening and contraction by default, but track the use of variables quantitatively in some algebraic structure – usually a semiring. We present Grass ( Gra ded and s ub s tructural), a type system which incorporates mechanisms from both of these approaches, thus allowing maximally flexible control over variable usage. Furthermore, Grass allows grades from an arbitrary collection of grade algebras to coexist in the same system, thus allowing different variables to be controlled with respect to different notions of resource within the same program. We develop the categorical semantics of Grass , and find that, on the level of categorical semantics, it subsumes multiple established systems such as LNL [ 4 ] , Adjoint Logic [ 27 ] , and mGL [ 31 ] .



keywords

Programming Languages,
Substructural Logic,
Graded Modal Types,
Categorical Semantics

1
Introduction

The treatment of program variables as resources has seen a rise over the last few
decades.
While classically variables were treated like propositions
which could be reused or discarded at will,
the resourceful treatment acknowledges that this is not true in
many common computing use cases.
Treating variables as resources allows programmers to verify interesting
correctness properties,
like the absence of memory leaks or use-after-free errors.

Two distinct approaches to the resourceful analysis of programs
exist in the literature.

Substructural logics

restrict weakening and contraction,
1
1

1

Exchange can
also be restricted to create non-commutative logics
[
18
]
, but we do
not consider this here.

prohibiting variables from being duplicated or discarded by default,
then selectively re-introducing these capabilities where needed.
In Linear Logic
[
15
]
, this is done via the of-course modality.
Benton generalized Linear Logic to Linear-non-Linear Logic
[
4
]
,
a system which features two sub-logics, one where assumptions are intuitionistic
and one where assumptions can be either intuitionistic or linear.
Pruiksma et al.
[
27
]
further generalized this system to
Adjoint Logic, allowing any number of logics, each with its own set of
permitted structural rules, to coexist in the same system.
This allows programmers to specify exactly which structural rules are applicable
to which variable in a program, independently of the other variables.

The second approach is to allow structural rules by default, but to
annotate assumptions with so called
grades
, usually drawn from a semiring.
In this approach, the control over the usage of
assumptions stems from the algebraic structure of the grades with graded contraction being
modeled by addition and weakening being modeled by an explicit zero element.
Graded systems are commonly parametrized by their grade structure and can
therefore model a variety of resource notions.
Examples of applications are:
Garbage collection
[
10
]
,
liveness analysis
[
26
]
,
security
[
1
,
12
,
20
,
22
,
23
]
,
dataflow analysis

[
26
,
30
,
29
]
,
numerical sensitivity tracking
[
3
,
11
]
,
and reasoning about probabilistic programs
[
28
]
.

In this work we present
Grass
, a type system which unifies these two approaches.
Our system allows grades drawn from any number of grade algebras
to be used simultaneously.
For example, we can type a function

\mathtt{read\_decrypt}\colon(k:^{\mathsf{Hi}}\mathtt{Key})\to(f:^{1}\mathtt{FileHandle})\to\mathtt{Text}
,
which reads the contents of an encrypted file
f
using the decryption key
k
.
The grade annotation
\mathsf{Hi}
ensures that the key is used in a secure context,
while the grade annotation
1
ensures that the file handle is properly
accessed and closed.
Our type system further allows control over graded contraction and weakening.
For example two file handles used linearly should not be contractible
into one which can be used twice.

We develop a categorical semantics for our type system,
based on graded linear exponential comonads
[
16
]
.
Our type system is assembled from a collection of graded logics connected by morphisms,
and this design is mirrored in the categorical semantics.
We develop a novel notion of morphisms between graded comonads
based on morphisms of actegories [sic]
[
9
]
.
We show that known categorical models for substructural logics
give rise to models of
Grass
in a straightforward way.
Thus, on the level of categorical semantics,

Grass
is capable of expressing both graded and substructural logics,
as well as combinations thereof.

2
Single-mode system

Grass
is paratemeterized by a collection of so called
modes
,
each of which admits different graded structural rules.
In this section we explain our novel weakening and contraction rules
in the situation when this collection is instantiated to consist of only one mode.
In this case,
Grass
simplifies to be an ordinary graded type theory,
nearly identical to the purely graded fragment of

mGL

[
31
]
,
except with modified weakening and contraction rules.
We will focus on the structural rules here;
the full set of typing rules is given in §
A

of the appendix.
Before we begin with our treatment of the structural rules,
we review weakening and contraction in existing graded systems,
then explain how we control them further using modes.
For contraction, the idea is to restrict it to a set of mutually contractible grades,
subject to some conditions.
For weakening the situation is simpler: It can just be toggled on or off.
We end this section with a discussion of morphisms of modes,
which capture the notion that one mode has more structure than another.

2.1
Weakening and contraction in existing systems

We review the behavior of graded weakening and contraction in existing systems.
These structural rules are present in most graded type systems,
either as explicit rules
[
13
,
25
,
26
,
31
]
,
as admissible rules

[
10
,
12
,
22
]
,
or even a mix of the two with an explicit weakening rule,
but admissible graded contraction

[
23
]
.
In this section we will consider typing judgments of the form
\rho\odot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{t}:\mathit{T}

where
t
is a term,
T
is a type and
\Gamma
a typing context.
Furthermore,
\rho
is a list of grades of the same length as
\Gamma
.
Each entry of
\rho
dictates the capabilties with which
the variable in the corresponding position in
\Gamma
may be used by
t
.
The graded contraction and weakening rules are now

\frac{\rho,\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A},\mathit{y}:\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{B}}{\rho,\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{z}:\mathit{A}\vdash[\mathit{z}/\mathit{x},\mathit{z}/\mathit{y}]\mathit{e}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{Cont}}{},}\and\frac{\rho\odot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{A}}{\rho,0\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{B}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{Weak}}{},}.

The contraction rule allows sharing a variable
across two sub-expressions in a term,
but also requires its capabilities to be split between the two expressions.
The weakening rule allows the introduction of additional variables into the context,
marked by the grade
0
.
The graded structural rules can be seen as generalizations of the structural
rules available using the of-course modality
!A
in Linear Logic,
where they correspond to maps

!A\multimap!A\otimes!A
and
!A\multimap I
respectively.
The graded structural rules generalize these to

\square_{q_{1}+q_{2}}A\multimap\square_{q_{1}}A\otimes\square_{q_{2}}A
and

\square_{0}A\multimap I
,
where the type
\square_{q}A
indicates use at grade
q
.
This allows fine grained tracking of the usage of each hypothesis.
Depending on the grade algebra being used,
the graded structural rules may simply correspond to the ones of linear logic,
but by choosing different kinds of semirings we can enforce different styles
of capability tracking.
For example,
if we use the natural numbers to grade our types we can track the precise number
of times variables are being used rather than simply one or zero.

2.2
Modes

In this section we formalize a novel structure used to control weakening and
contraction in the graded setting, which we call
modes
.
The main insight is that contraction can be controlled by specifying
a set of mutually contractible grades called an
ideal
.
The notion of ideals is borrowed from ring theory,
where ideals are a well-known concept (see e.g. Lang
[
19
]
).

Definition 2.1
.

A
semiring
is a set
R
together with binary operations

(+),(\cdot)\colon R\times R\to R

and elements
0,1\in R
such that

(R,+,0)
is a commutative monoid,

(R,\cdot,1)
is a monoid
and such that
for all
x,y,z\in R

we have

x\cdot(y+z)=(x\cdot y)+(x\cdot z)
and

(x+y)\cdot z=(x\cdot z)+(y\cdot z)
and

0\cdot x=0=x\cdot 0
.
A
preordered semiring
is a semiring equipped with a
preorder
(\leq)
such that
(+)
and
(\cdot)

are monotone with respect to
(\leq)
, i.e.
whenever
x\leq x^{\prime}
and
y\leq y^{\prime}
, then
x+y\leq x^{\prime}+y^{\prime}

and likewise for
(\cdot)
.
Preordered semirings are also called
grade algebras

and their elements are called
grades
.

Example 2.2
.

(1)

The set of natural numbers
\mathbb{N}
with the usual arithmetic operations
forms a semiring.
There are several natural choices of preorder on
\mathbb{N}
:
The usual ordering
0\leq 1\leq\mathellipsis
,
its opposite
0\geq 1\geq\mathellipsis
,
and the
discrete ordering

n\leq m\iff n=m
.

(2)

The set
\{0,1\}
can be equipped with two distinct semiring structures,
by either defining
1+1=0
or
1+1=1
.
Only the latter structure will be of interest to us.
There are three interesting preorders,
defined by either
0\leq 1
or
0\geq 1
,
or the discrete ordering.

(3)

The
none-one-tons
semiring is the set
\{0,1,\omega\}
.
Addition and multiplication are fixed by the semiring axioms and the equations

1+1=1+\omega=\omega+\omega=\omega\cdot\omega=\omega
.
Again, there are multiple possible preorders,
for example, the preorder generated by
0\leq\omega

or the one generated by
0\leq\omega\geq 1
.

(4)

There is a unique semiring with
0=1
,
which we denote
\top
.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
R
be a semiring.
A
(two sided) ideal
in
R
is a subset
I\subseteq R
such that

0\in I
,
and whenever
x,y\in I
, then
x+y\in I
,
and for any
x\in I
and
r\in R
,
we have
r\cdot x\in I
and
x\cdot r\in I
.

Example 2.4
.

(1)

Any semiring
R
admits the trivial ideals
\{0\}\subseteq R

and
R\subseteq R
.

(2)

The sets
\{0,\omega\}\subseteq\{0,1,\omega\}

and
\mathbb{N}\setminus\{1\}\subseteq\mathbb{N}

are ideals.

(3)

Given a family of ideals
I_{j}
,
the intersection
\bigcap_{j}I_{j}
is again an ideal.
Hence, given any subset
X\subseteq R
there exists a smallest ideal
containing
X
:
There exists an ideal containing
X

since
R\subseteq R
is an ideal itself.
Now consider the set of all such ideals and take their intersection.

Definition 2.5
.

A mode
\mathsf{m}

is a triple
(R_{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m}),\mathsf{Weak}(\mathsf{m}))

where
R_{\mathsf{m}}
is a grade algebra,

\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})\subseteq R_{\mathsf{m}}
is an ideal
and
\mathsf{Weak}(\mathsf{m})\in\{\mathsf{true},\mathsf{false}\}
.

Remark 2.6
.

Our notion of modes is a generalization of the notion of modes given in
Adjoint Logic
[
27
]
.
There, a mode is equipped with two boolean values,
indicating whether weakening and contraction are permitted.
In the graded setting,
we can control weakening and contraction more granularly
by enabling them only for a subset of grades.
Contraction is modeled by a set of mutually contractible grades.
We will see below how the conditions that this set is an ideal arise
naturally in the syntax.
While it may appear that weakening is still controlled by a boolean,
the situation is a bit more subtle:
Most graded systems, including
Grass
, have a subsumption rule,
which states that grades need only be upper bounds on the actual usage
of variables.
Thus, when weakening is enabled,
unused variables can be introduced at any grade
q\geq 0
,
by applying weakening followed by subsumption.
We do not require
0
to be the bottom element of a grade algebra,
so weakening is controlled by the predicate
\mathsf{Weak}(\mathsf{m})

and the choice of preorder.

Example 2.7
.

The following modes will serve as running examples:

\mathsf{U}=(\top,\top,\mathsf{true})
,

\mathsf{R}=(\top,\top,\mathsf{false})
,

\mathsf{A}=(\{0,1\},\{0\},\mathsf{true})

and
\mathsf{L}=(\mathbb{N},\{0\},\mathsf{false})
.
We will see in Example
2.8

how these modes can recover intuitionistic, relevant,
affine and linear logic respectively.

2.3
Restricted structural rules

We can now describe the controlled structural of
Grass
.
Fix a mode
\mathsf{m}=(R_{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m}),\mathsf{Weak}(\mathsf{m}))
.
The new rules for contraction and weakening are as follows:

\frac{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\in\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})\quad\rho,\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A},\mathit{y}:\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{B}}{\rho,\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{z}:\mathit{A}\vdash[\mathit{z}/\mathit{x},\mathit{z}/\mathit{y}]\mathit{e}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{S.Cont}}{},}\and\frac{\mathsf{Weak}(\mathsf{m})\quad\rho\odot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{A}}{\rho,0\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{B}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{S.Weak}}{},}.

The weakening rule is straightforwardly modified:
It can be toggled on or off by the mode.
Contraction is only allowed when both grades are elements of
\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})
.
The requirement that
\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})
is an ideal arises naturally as follows.
First, consider a judgment

\rho,\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{3}}}}:\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{t}:\mathit{B}

where
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\in\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})
.
We want
\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})
to act as a set of mutually contractible grades,
so it should be possible to contract this into a term

\rho,\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}+\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{z}:\mathit{A}\vdash[\mathit{z}/\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{z}/\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\mathit{z}/\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{3}}}}]\mathit{t}:\mathit{B}
.
Since the contraction rule only allows contracting two variables at a time,
we must pick an order:
We either contract
x_{1}
with
x_{2}
and then the result with
x_{3}
,
or
x_{2}
and
x_{3}
first and then the result with
x_{1}
.
The condition that
\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})
is closed under addition means that
both orderings are always possible,
and in fact the resulting derivations should be considered equal.
Similarly,
the condition that
0\in\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})
ensures that
contraction interacts well with weakening in the sense that
for
\mathit{q}\in\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})
we have the derivation

\frac{\frac{\rho,\mathit{q}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{B}}{\rho,\mathit{q},0\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A},\mathit{y}:\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{Right = \text{{S.Weak}{}}}}{\rho,\mathit{q}+0\odot\Gamma,\mathit{z}:\mathit{A}\vdash[\mathit{z}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{e}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{Right = \text{{S.Cont}{}}}

and the resulting term should be considered equal to
e
.
Finally, contraction should also interact well with substitution.
In graded type systems, substitution takes the form

\frac{\rho,\mathit{r}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{B}\and\sigma\odot\Delta\vdash\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}}{\rho,\mathit{r}\cdot\sigma\odot\Gamma,\Delta\vdash[\mathit{t}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{e}:\mathit{B}}.

Given terms

\rho,\mathit{r}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{B}

and

\sigma,\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\odot\Delta,\mathit{z_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{T},\mathit{z_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{T}\vdash\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}

with
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\in\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})

we can apply contraction, obtaining

\sigma,\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\odot\Delta,\mathit{z}:\mathit{T}\vdash[\mathit{z}/\mathit{z_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{z}/\mathit{z_{{\mathrm{2}}}}]\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}

then perform substitution.
The condition that
\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})
is an ideal ensures that the other ordering
is possible too:
If we substitute first, obtaining

\rho,\mathit{r}\cdot\sigma,\mathit{r}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{r}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\odot\Gamma,\Delta,\mathit{z_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{T},\mathit{z_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{T}\vdash[\mathit{t}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{e}:\mathit{B}

then we may still apply contraction.
Note that both orders of these operations result in the same term.

Example 2.8
.

We can recover non-graded logics by using appropriate modes.

(1)

Using the mode
\mathsf{U}=(\top,\top,\mathsf{true})

we recover intuitionistic logic:
Contraction and weakening are permitted.

(2)

Using mode
\mathsf{R}=(\top,\top,\mathsf{false})

recovers
relevant logic
:
There is only one grade that may be contracted with itself,
so variables may be reused arbitrarily.
However, weakening is disabled, so unused variables are not allowed.

(3)

Using the mode
\mathsf{A}=(\{0,1\},\{0\},\mathsf{true})

with ordering
0\leq 1
, we recover
affine logic
.
Variables graded
1
may be unused by applying a combination of the weakening
and subsumption rules.
Contraction is only allowed for unused variables graded
0
,
so reuse is impossible.

(4)

Using the mode
(\{0,1\},\{0\},\mathsf{false})
we almost recover linear logic.
Variables graded
1
are guaranteed to have linear usage.
The system we obtain here is not exactly linear logic (hence “almost”),
since variables graded
0
can still be introduced explicitly using
the graded modality
\square_{0}A
,
or by applying the elimination rule for the unit type.
(The unit type has no computational content so its terms may be eliminated
at any grade.)

(5)

Similarly, grading by the mode
\mathsf{L}=(\mathbb{N},\{0\},\mathsf{false})

with discrete ordering
tracks the number of free occurences of variables in a term exactly.
Since contraction and weakening are disabled,
variables graded by
n\neq 1
must be introduced explicitly
via the graded modality
\square_{n}A
,
or the unit type’s elimination rule.
Thus, we recover another variant on linear logic.

Example 2.9
.

Using the mode
(R=\{0,1,\omega\},\{0,\omega\},\mathsf{true})
,
the grades mean unused
(0)
, linear
(1)
, and unrestricted
(\omega)
.
Furthermore, two variables graded
1
may not be contracted into one variable
graded
1+1=\omega
.
This is useful, for example, when dealing with file handles:
Two file handles used linearly cannot be replaced by one file handle with
unrestricted use.
Similar considerations apply to the mode

(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}\setminus\{1\},\mathsf{true})
:
Contraction in only allowed for variables which are known to be used non-linearly.

2.4
Comparing modes

Definition 2.10
.

A morphism of semirings
R\to S
is a function

f\colon R\to S
such that

f(0)=0
,
f(1)=1
, and for all
x,y\in R
,

f(x+y)=f(x)+f(y)
and
f(x\cdot y)=f(x)\cdot f(y)
.
A morphism of grade algebras
f\colon R\to S
is a morphism of
semirings which is also monotone, i.e.
x\leq y
implies
f(x)\leq f(y)
.
A morphism of modes

\mathsf{m}\to\mathsf{n}
is a morphism

f\colon R_{\mathsf{m}}\to R_{\mathsf{n}}
of the underlying grade algebras
such that for each
x\in\Cont(\mathsf{m})
, we have
f(x)\in\Cont(\mathsf{n})

and such that the boolean formula
\Weak(\mathsf{m})\to\Weak(\mathsf{n})
is true.
This definition yields a category of modes, denoted
\bf{Mode}
.

A morphism of modes
\varphi\colon\mathsf{m}\to\mathsf{n}
implies
that
\mathsf{n}
has “more structure” than
\mathsf{m}
in an appropriate way.
We give some intuition for this:
Given a derivable judgment

\rho\odot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}
under mode
\mathsf{m}

there exists a corresponding judgment

\varphi(\rho)\and\Gamma\vdash t:A

which is derivable under mode
\mathsf{n}
.
Here,
\varphi(\rho)
is the result of applying
\varphi
to each element of
\rho
.
In other words, there is a translation function.
This translation is not quite the identity on types and terms,
since some types and terms feature grade annotations,
which also need to be transported along
\varphi
.
The proof that this translation is sound is straightforward by induction,
since morphisms of modes preserve all structure carried by a mode.
In particular, each application of the weakening or contraction rules
at mode
\mathsf{m}
can be mirrored at
\mathsf{n}
,
which we take to mean that
\mathsf{n}
has more structure than
\mathsf{m}
.
This intuition has two interesing special cases:
The category
\bf{Mode}
has a terminal object
\mathsf{U}

and an initial object
\mathsf{L}
.
We already saw in Example
2.8

that these modes recover intuitionistic and linear logic respectively.
Therefore it makes sense to think of the terminal and initial modes as
having maximal and minimal structure respectively.

3
Syntax of
Grass

In this section we explain the syntax and typing rules of
Grass
.
Our system is parametrized by a family of modes,
with each type belonging to one mode.
Like with Adjoint Logic
[
27
]
,
the modes are arranged in a preorder,
with modes higher in the order admitting more structural rules than those
lower in the order, as discussed in §
2.4
.
First, we present several definitions used throughout the paper.

Definition 3.1
.

Grass
is parametrized by a functor

S\to\bf{Mode}
, where
S
is some preordered set.
We usually elide mention of this functor, instead thinking of its image
as a preordered set of modes
\mathsf{m},\mathsf{n},\mathsf{l},\mathellipsis
together with a coherent
system of morphisms
\varphi_{\mathsf{m},\mathsf{n}}\colon\mathsf{m}\to\mathsf{n}
,
whenever
\mathsf{m}\leq\mathsf{n}
.
We fix such data for the remainder of this text.

Definition 3.2
.

Grades are denoted by the letters
\mathit{q},\mathit{r},\mathit{s}
.
They may be drawn from any of the grade algebras
R_{\mathsf{m}}
.

Grade vectors
are finite lists of grades, denoted by the letters

\rho
,
\sigma
,
\tau
.
Grade vectors are
heterogenous
, i.e. entries of one grade vector
may be drawn from more than one grade algebra.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\mathsf{m}\leq\mathsf{n}
be modes
and let
q\in R_{\mathsf{m}}
and
r\in R_{\mathsf{n}}
.
We define

\mathit{q}\mathit{r}=\varphi_{\mathsf{m},\mathsf{n}}(q)\cdot r\in R_{\mathsf{n}}
.
We extend this notation to scalar multiplication:
If
q\in R_{\mathsf{m}}
and
\rho=(\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{r_{\mathit{k}}})

with
r_{i}\in R_{\mathsf{m}_{i}}
and
\mathsf{m}_{\mathit{i}}\geq\mathsf{m}
for
i\in\{1,\mathellipsis,k\}
,
then we write

\mathit{q}\rho=(\mathit{q}\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{q}\mathit{r_{\mathit{k}}})=(\varphi_{\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{1}}(q)\cdot r_{1},\mathellipsis,\varphi_{\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{k}}(q)\cdot r_{k})
.

Definition 3.4
.

Types are denoted
A,B,C,T
.
Each type in
Grass
belongs to a unique mode and
we write
\mathit{A}\in\mathsf{Type}(\mathsf{m})

to indicate that the type
A
is well-formed and belongs to mode
\mathsf{m}
.
In this case, variables of type
A
are graded by grades drawn from
R_{\mathsf{m}}
,
and structural rules may be applied to them as prescribed by
\Weak(\mathsf{m})

and
\Cont(\mathsf{m})
.
The rules for well-formed types are given in Figure
1
.

Figure 3:
Grass

\beta\eta
-conversions

Definition 3.5
.

Typing judgments in
Grass
have the form

\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\odot\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}\mathit{t}\colon\mathit{T}
.
The roles of
\Gamma
,
\mathit{t}
, and
\mathit{T}
are as before.
There are two new components in the judgment:
The annotation
\mathsf{m}
on the turnstile indicates that
\mathit{T}\in\mathsf{Type}(\mathsf{m})
,
and we say that this typing judgment is
made at mode
\mathsf{m}
.
The other new component is
\mathsf{M}
, which is a list of modes,
also called a
mode vector
.
If
\rho=(\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{r_{\mathit{k}}})

and
\mathsf{M}=(\mathsf{m}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathsf{m}_{\mathit{k}})

and
\Gamma=\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{x_{\mathit{k}}}:\mathit{A_{\mathit{k}}}
,
then the above judgment indicates that
\mathit{A_{\mathit{i}}}\in\mathsf{Type}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathit{i}})

and that the variable
x_{i}
is used with capabilities indicated by
r_{i}
.

Definition 3.6
.

Typing contexts are denoted by
\Gamma
,
\Delta
.
When
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are contexts, their concatenation is written

\Gamma,\Delta
and is only well-defined when the contexts
\Gamma

and
\Delta
have no variables in common.
Whenever we use context concatenation, we implicitly assume that this condition
is satisfied.

In any derivable typing judgment
\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\odot\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}\mathit{t}\colon\mathit{T}
,
each mode
\mathsf{n}
occuring in
\mathsf{M}
must satisfy
\mathsf{n}\geq\mathsf{m}
,
which we write concisely as
\mathsf{M}\geq\mathsf{m}
.
A similar requirement is present in Linear-non-Linear Logic
[
4
]
,
where linear terms may depend on non-linear variables, but not vice-versa,
and was also observed by Pruiksma et al.
[
27
]
,
where it is called
independence
.
The typing rules of
Grass
are set up to ensure that independence
holds in all derivable judgments.
In
Grass
, independence also ensures that all scalar multiplications
occuring in the typing rules are well-defined
(cf. Definition
3.3
).
We discuss the typing rules in detail in the following paragraphs.

We begin with the structural rules.
The grade
1
corresponds to linear usage,
and is thus used in the variable rule (
Var
).
The rules for weakening and contraction are as before,
only modified to account for the presence of different modes.
Contraction (rule
Cont
)
is only allowed between grades of the same mode.
In the weakening rule (
Weak
)
the condition
\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}
ensures that independence holds in the conclusion,
if it holds in the assumption.
We assume an implicit exchange rule,
which allows reordering of contexts.
When this occurs, the grade and mode vectors must be reordered accordingly.

The type
\operatorname{\downarrow}^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}}\mathit{A}
is defined for
\mathit{A}\in\mathsf{Type}(\mathsf{m})
,

q\in R_{\mathsf{m}}
and
\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}
(rule
Ty.Drop
).
Terms of this type behave like terms of type
A
,
with the added constraint that they must be used at grade
q
.
Furthermore, the type
\operatorname{\downarrow}^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}}\mathit{A}
belongs to mode
\mathsf{n}
and is therefore
subject to the structural rules imposed by
\mathsf{n}
.
The introduction rule (
Drop.i
)
promotes a term
t
of type
A
to a term
\operatorname{\downarrow}^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}}\mathit{t}
of
type
\operatorname{\downarrow}^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}}\mathit{A}
.
The resources used to produce
t
are multiplied by
q
during this promotion,
reflecting the
q
-fold reuse later.
The elimination rule (
Drop.e
)
uses a let binding
\mathbin{\mathsf{let}}_{@\mathit{q}}\operatorname{\downarrow}_{\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}}\mathit{x}=\mathit{e}\mathbin{\mathsf{in}}\mathit{t}

to extract a term of type
A
from
e
,
and make it available to the body
t
,
which then must use that term at grade
q
.
This type combines two distinct feature sets from existing literature:
First, some graded type theories
(e.g.
[
1
,
8
,
10
,
13
,
23
]
)
include a graded modality, commonly written
\square_{q}A
.
We can recover such a type in
Grass
by setting

\square_{q}A:=\operatorname{\downarrow}^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathsf{m}\leq\mathsf{m}}\mathit{A}
.
Second,
\operatorname{\downarrow}^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}}\mathit{A}
is
an analogue of
\mathsf{F}A
from LNL
[
4
]
,
and
\mathsf{Grd}_{q}A
from
mGL

[
31
]
,
since these constructs move types to a mode with fewer structural rules.
Our notation mirrors that of Pruiksma et al.
[
27
]
.

The type
\operatorname{\uparrow}_{\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}}\mathit{A}
is defined for
\mathit{A}\in\mathsf{Type}(\mathsf{n})

and
\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}
(rule
Ty.Raise
).
The introduction rule (
Raise.i
) constructs
a term
\operatorname{\uparrow}_{\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}}\mathit{e}:\operatorname{\uparrow}_{\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}}\mathit{A}
from a term
e:A
.
The produced term and its type belong to mode
\mathsf{m}
and thus have fewer
structural restrictions imposed upon them.
The elimination rule (
Raise.e
) allows us to extract
a term of type
A
from a term of type
\operatorname{\uparrow}_{\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}}\mathit{A}
directly.
The type
\operatorname{\uparrow}_{\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}}\mathit{A}
is the analogue of
\mathsf{G}A
from LNL
and
\mathsf{Lin}
from
mGL
.

Grass
includes a subsumption rule
Sub
.
This rule means that a grade vector
\rho

in a judgment
\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\odot\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}\mathit{t}\colon\mathit{A}
need only be an upper bound on the
actual usage of the variables in
\Gamma
within the term
\mathit{e}
.
The relation
\rho\leq\sigma
is defined inductively by

\frac{}{\emptyset\leq\emptyset}\and\frac{\rho\leq\sigma\and\mathit{r}\in R_{\mathsf{m}}\and\mathit{q}\in R_{\mathsf{m}}\and\mathit{r}\leq\mathit{q}}{(\rho,\mathit{r})\leq(\sigma,\mathit{q})}.

When the preorder is discrete, this rule is effectively disabled.

For each mode
\mathsf{m}
, we have a unit type
\mathbf{I}_{\mathsf{m}}

(rule
Ty.Unit
)
which admits a closed term
\star_{\mathsf{m}}

that can be produced without using any resources
(rule
Unit.i
).
Terms of type
\mathbf{I}_{\mathsf{m}}
hold no computational information,
and may thus be eliminated (rule
Unit.e
) at any grade
q

by the pattern matching construct
\mathbin{\mathsf{let}}_{@\mathit{q}}\star_{\mathsf{m}}=\mathit{e}\mathbin{\mathsf{in}}\mathit{t}
.

The tensor product type
\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\otimes\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
is defined only when the types
A_{1}

and
A_{2}
belong to the same mode.
Its introduction rule (
Pair.i
) is standard,
allowing the construction of a pair
(\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}})
from terms
\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}

and
\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
.
The pair elimination rule (
Pair.e
) functions by pattern
matching.
It asserts that using a pair at grade
q
is the same thing as using each of its
components separately at grade
q
.
Since term reuse is modeled by multiplication,
the grades used to produce the pair are multiplied by
q

as part of the elimination rule.

The function type
\mathit{A}^{\mathit{q}:\mathsf{m}}\multimap\mathit{B}
is defined when
\mathit{A}\in\mathsf{Type}(\mathsf{m})

and
\mathit{B}\in\mathsf{Type}(\mathsf{n})
with
\mathsf{m}\geq\mathsf{n}
.
The requirement that
\mathsf{m}\geq\mathsf{n}
is an instance of independence.
The grade annotation
q
indicates how functions of this type use their parameters.
Functions are introduced (rule
Fun.i
)
using
\lambda
-abstraction.
The grade annotation
q
corresponds to the
the use of the abstacted variable in the function body.
Elimination (
Fun.e
) is done by function application.
Since grade multiplication corresponds to reuse,
the grades used to produce the parameter
e
are multiplied by
q

during application.

Like the product, the sum type
\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\oplus\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
is defined between types
of the same mode. Its introduction forms (
Sum.il
,
Sum.ir
)
are standard, injecting a term
e
of type
A_{1}
(resp.
A_{2}
) into the sum.
These injections do not affect the grades used to produce
e
.
Elimination occurs by cases: to use a term of type
\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\oplus\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
at grade
q
,
we must specify two branches,
which use a term of
A_{1}
(resp.
A_{2}
) at grade
q
.

Example 3.7
.

Let
\mathsf{m}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathsf{m}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
be two modes, and let
\mathsf{L}
be the initial mode
as discussed in §
2.4
.
Instantiate
Grass
with modes
\mathsf{m}_{1}>\mathsf{L}<\mathsf{m}_{2}
.
Write
\square^{q}_{\mathsf{m}}A=\operatorname{\downarrow}^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{m}}(\operatorname{\uparrow}_{\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{m}}\mathit{A})
.
Then
\mathsf{L}
behaves like linear logic,
with two graded modalities
\square^{q}_{\mathsf{m}_{1}}A
and
\square^{q}_{\mathsf{m}_{2}}A

which can account for two distinct notions of resource usage.
This is a generalization of
mGL
allowing grades from two different
grade algebras.

Example 3.8
.

Using the modes
\mathsf{L}<\mathsf{U}

of Example
2.8
,
we obtain LNL
[
4
]
.
If we also use the modes
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{R}
,
we obtain a combined system in the style of Adjoint Logic

[
27
]
.

Theorem 3.9

(Substitution)
.

If
\rho,\mathit{r}\mid\mathsf{M},\mathsf{m}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash_{\mathsf{n}}\mathit{e}\colon\mathit{B}

and

\sigma\mid\mathsf{N}\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}\mathit{t}\colon\mathit{A}
,
then

\rho,\mathit{r}\sigma\mid\mathsf{M},\mathsf{N}\odot\Gamma,\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{n}}[\mathit{t}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{e}\colon\mathit{B}

Proof 3.10
.

See §
B.1
in the appendix.

We include a set of
\beta
- and
\eta
-conversion rules
which are given in Figure
3
.
The
\beta
-rules are mostly standard:
Pattern matching eliminators reduce when the scrutinee of the match
is a constructor for the respective type.
In the pattern matching form for the unit type,
the grade annotation
q
gets ignored since the term
\star_{\mathsf{m}}

carries no computational information.
Function applications reduce by substitution into the function body,
when the function in question is a lambda.
The type
\operatorname{\uparrow}_{\mathsf{m}\leq\mathsf{n}}\mathit{A}
is a negative type with an explicit destructor

\operatorname{\uparrow}^{-1}_{\mathsf{m}\leq\mathsf{n}}\mathit{e}
, rather than a pattern matching one,
which acts as a left inverse to the constructor
\operatorname{\uparrow}_{\mathsf{m}\leq\mathsf{n}}\mathit{e}
.
The
\eta
-rules are similarly standard: eliminating then reconstructing a term
recovers that term.
These conversions preserve typing:

Theorem 3.11

(Type preservation)
.

The
\beta
- and
\eta
-conversions preserve typing and grading, i.e.

(1)

Whenever
\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\odot\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv_{\beta}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\colon\mathit{A}
,
then
\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\odot\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\colon\mathit{A}
,
and
\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\odot\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\colon\mathit{A}
.

(2)

Whenever
\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\odot\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv_{\eta}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\colon\mathit{A}
,
then
\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\odot\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\colon\mathit{A}
,
and
\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\odot\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\colon\mathit{A}
.

Proof 3.12
.

See §§
B.2
,
B.3
in the appendix.

4
Categorical semantics

We present our categorical semantics.
The structure of the categorical models mirrors that of the syntax:

Grass
is composed out of several graded logics, one for each mode,
tied together by a system of modalities
\operatorname{\downarrow}^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}}\mathit{A}

and
\operatorname{\uparrow}_{\mathsf{m}\leq\mathsf{n}}\mathit{A}
mediating between them.
Similiarly, a model for
Grass
consists of several models for ordinary
graded logic, tied together by an appropriate system of morphisms,
here realized as adjunctions with some additional structure.
To appropriately capture the modified weakening and contraction rules,
we must make some changes relative to the existing literature.
Because of this, we set up all necessary terminology from scratch.
The categorical semantics presented here do not include the sum type;
Adding the necessary structure to model it is straightforward.
The following definitions are adapted from Katsumata
[
16
]
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\mathsf{m}=(R_{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m}),\mathsf{Weak}(\mathsf{m}))
be a mode.
The preorder on
R_{\mathsf{m}}
equips the sets
R_{\mathsf{m}}

and
\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})
with categorical structures.
We write
(R_{\mathsf{m}},\cdot,1)
for the symmetric monoidal structure
on
R_{\mathsf{m}}
whose tensor product is multiplication
and tensor unit is
1\in R_{\mathsf{m}}
.
Similarly, we write
(\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m}),+,0)
for the symmetric monoidal
structure on
\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})
whose tensor product is addition
and tensor unit is
0\in\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})
.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,I)
be a symmetric monoidal category.
Write
\SMC(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
for the category of strong symmetric monoidal
endofunctors on
\mathcal{C}
and monoidal natural transformations between them.
This category can be equipped with a monoidal structure in two ways:

(1)

Pointwise:
For
F,G\in\SMC(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
,
their tensor product is

(F\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}G)(X)=F(X)\otimes G(X)
.
The tensor unit is the constant functor sending everything to
I\in\mathcal{C}
.
We denote this structure
(\SMC(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}),\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}},\hat{I})
.

(2)

By composition:
The tensor product is composition of functors,
and the tensor unit is the identity functor.
We denote this structure

(\SMC(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}),\circ,\id)
.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal category,
and let
\mathsf{m}=(R_{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m}),\mathsf{Weak}(\mathsf{m}))
be a mode.
An
exponential action
(of
\mathsf{m}
on
\mathcal{C}
) is a functor

D\colon R^{{\mathrm{op}}}_{\mathsf{m}}\to\SMC(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})

equipped with the following additional structure.

(1)

The structure of a colax monoidal functor with signature

(R_{\mathsf{m}}^{\mathrm{op}},\cdot,1)\to(\SMC(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}),\circ,\id)

on
D
, i.e. natural transformations

\delta_{r,q}\colon D(r\cdot q)\to D(r)\circ D(q)

and
\epsilon\colon D(1)\to\id
.

(2)

For
r,q\in\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})
, a natural transformation

c_{r,q}\colon D(r+q)\to D(r)\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}D(q)
.
Making the following diagram commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D((r_{1}+r_{2})+r_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(r_{1}+r_{2})\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}D(r_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D(r_{1})\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}D(r_{2}))\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}D(r_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(r_{1}+(r_{2}+r_{3}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(r_{1})\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}D(r_{2}+r_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(r_{1})\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}(D(r_{2})\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}D(r_{3}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{r_{1}+r_{2},r_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{r_{1},r_{2}}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{r_{1},r_{2}+r_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}c_{r_{2},r_{3}}}

Furthermore, if
\mathsf{Weak}(\mathsf{m})
is true, a morphism
w\colon D(0)\to\hat{I}
.
In this case, the transformations
c_{r,q}
and
w
are required
to assemble into a colax monoidal structure on the functor

r\mapsto D(r)\colon(\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})^{{\mathrm{op}}},+,0)\to\SMC(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}).

Note that the requirement that this functor is colax monoidal
already includes the above diagram.
We include that diagram explicitly, since we cannot require that this functor
be colax monoidal in the absence of the morphism
w
.

These structures are related by the diagrams of Figure

4
,
which are required to commute whenever all morphisms in them are defined.
For the first two diagrams this means that
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\in\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})

and for the last two diagrams this means that
\mathsf{Weak}(\mathsf{m})
is true.
We write
\tau_{r,X,Y}\colon D(r)(X)\otimes D(r)(Y)\to D(r)(X\otimes Y)
and
\iota_{r}\colon I\to D(r)(I)

for the strong monoidal structure on
D(r)
.
Furthermore, the diagrams in Figure
4

use the infix notation
D(r)(X)=:r\and X
,
which we will use for the remainder of this text.

Example 4.4
.

We consider exponential actions of the modes given in
Example
2.8
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be an SMCC.

(1)

Any SMCC admits an exponential action of the initial mode
\mathsf{L}

by setting
n\and X=X^{\otimes n}
.
Similarly, any SMCC admits an exponential action of the mode

(\{0,1\},\{0\},\mathsf{false})
, by setting
0\and X=I

and
1\and X=X
.

(2)

An exponential action of the terminal mode
\mathsf{U}

is equivalent to specifying the structure of a Linear Category

[
7
]
on
\mathcal{C}
.
In other words, an SMCC with an exponential action of the terminal mode
is precisely a model of Intuitionistic Linear Logic, with
the exponential action modelling the of-course modality.
If we choose
1\and(-)=\id
,
the action equips
\mathcal{C}
with “projections and diagonals”
and hence
\mathcal{C}
is a cartesian closed category.
2
2

2

This result is folklore;
A proof is given in Melliès
[
21
]
.

(3)

An exponential action of the mode
\mathsf{R}
on
\mathcal{C}

is a functor
D
together with diagonal maps
DX\to DX\otimes DX
.
If we fix
D=\id
,
then
\mathcal{C}
becomes equipped with the structure of a

relevant category

[
24
]
.
Conversely, any relevant category admits an exponential action of
\mathsf{R}

by setting
1\and(-)=\id
.

(4)

If the tensor unit
I
is the terminal object of
\mathcal{C}
,
then
\mathcal{C}
admits an exponential action of the mode
\mathsf{A}

by setting
1\and(-)=\id
and
0\and(-)=\hat{I}
.
Conversely, an action of
\mathsf{A}
on
\mathcal{C}
with
1\and(-)=\id

and
0\and(-)=\hat{I}
exhibits the tensor unit as the terminal object
of
\mathcal{C}
.

All in all, we see that known models of intuitionistic, linear, relevant and affine
logic admit canonical exponential actions of the modes
\mathsf{U},\mathsf{L},\mathsf{R}

and
\mathsf{A}
respectively.

Figure 4:
Graded comonad coherence conditions

Definition 4.5
.

By a
symmetric lax monoidal adjunction

we mean a symmetric lax monoidal functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
between SMCs
together with a lax monoidal right adjoint
G
,
such that the unit and counit of the adjunction are monoidal natural transformations.
We concisely denote this by
F\dashv G\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
.
Recall that
F
is strong monoidal in this case
[
17
]
.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
\varphi\colon\mathsf{m}\to\mathsf{n}
be a morphism of modes,
and let
\and_{\mathsf{m}}

and
\and_{\mathsf{n}}

be exponential actions of
\mathsf{m}
,
\mathsf{n}

on the SMCCs
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{m}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{n}}

respectively.
A morphism of actions (over
\varphi
)
consists of a symmetric lax monoidal adjunction

F\dashv G\colon\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{n}}\to\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{m}}

together with a monoidal natural transformation

\ell_{r,A}\colon\varphi(r)\and_{\mathsf{n}}G(A)\to G(r\and_{\mathsf{m}}A)

satisfying the coherence conditions given in Figure
5
.
We define another natural transformation

\mu_{r,A}\colon F(\varphi(r)\and A)\to r\and F(A)

as the transpose of the composite

\varphi(r)\and A\xrightarrow{\varphi(r)\and\eta}\varphi(r)\and GFA\xrightarrow{\ell}G(r\and FA).

Morphisms between exponential actions compose in a straightforward way,
giving rise to a category
\bf{ExpAct}
of exponential actions,
whose objects are triples
(\mathsf{m},\mathcal{C},\and)
consisting of a mode
\mathsf{m}
,
an SMCC
\mathcal{C}
and an exponential action of
\mathsf{m}
on
\mathcal{C}
,
and whose morphisms are quadruples
(\varphi,F,G,\ell)
, where

\varphi
is a morphism of modes and
(F,G,\ell)

is a morphism of actions over
\varphi
.
We endow
\bf{ExpAct}
with the structure of a 2-category:
A
2
-cell between
(\varphi,F,G,\ell)
and
(\varphi,F^{\prime},G^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})

(note that the underlying morphisms of modes are required to be identical)
is a natural isomorphism
\alpha\colon G\to G^{\prime}
which respects the lineators
in the obvious way:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi(r)\and GA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(r\and A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi(r)\and G^{\prime}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}(r\and A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi(r)\and\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}

Figure 5:
Coherence laws on morphisms of exponential actions.
Unlabelled arrows are the lax monoidal structure on
G
.

Remark 4.7
.

The terminology
lineator
is borrowed from the theory of actegories [sic].
In fact, our notion of morphisms between actions is adapted from the notion
of morphisms between actegories.
See e.g. Cappucci and Gavranović
[
9
]
.

Remark 4.8
.

Let

(\varphi,F,G,\ell)\colon(\mathsf{m},\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{m}},\odot_{\mathsf{m}})\to(\mathsf{n},\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{n}},\odot_{\mathsf{n}})

be a morphism in
\bf{ExpAct}
.
Transposing
\ell
along the adjunction
F\dashv G
yields a
natural transformation

F(\varphi(-)\and_{\mathsf{n}}G(=))\to(-)\and_{\mathsf{m}}(=)
.
Katsumata proved that the domain of this
transformation satisfies the coherence conditions for exponential actions

[
16
]
.
In Katsumata’s setting, this functor is in fact a graded linear exponential comonad.
Here, it may fail to be an exponential action
since
G
is not required to be strong monoidal.
Thus,
\ell
is equivalent to a comparison morphism of the two actions
on
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{m}}
that naturally arise from the adjunction
F\dashv G
.

Lemma 4.9
.

The coherence conditions on
\ell
given in
Figure
5

are equivalent to the analogous coherence conditions on
\mu
given in
Figure
6
.

Proof 4.10
.

Straightforward manipulation of diagrams,
using the fact that
\ell
can be written as the composite

\varphi(r)\and G(A)\xrightarrow{\eta}GF(\varphi(r)\and G(A))\xrightarrow{G(\mu)}G(r\and FG(A))\xrightarrow{G(r\and\epsilon)}G(r\and A)
.
Here
\epsilon
is the counit of the adjunction
F\dashv G
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
(G,F,\ell)
and
(G^{\prime},F^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
be morphisms of actions over
\varphi
,
and
\alpha\colon G\to G^{\prime}
a 2-cell in
\bf{ExpAct}
.
Then
\alpha
induces a natural isomorphism
\beta\colon F^{\prime}\to F

which respects
\mu
in the obvious way:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}(\varphi(q)\and A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\and F^{\prime}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\varphi(q)\and A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\and F(A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\and\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{\prime}}

Definition 4.12
.

Recall that
Grass
is parametrized by a functor
d\colon S\to\bf{Mode}

where
S
is some preordered set.
We have a projection functor

p\colon\bf{ExpAct}\to\bf{Mode}
given by

(\mathsf{m},\mathcal{C},\and)\mapsto\mathsf{m}
.
A categorial model for
Grass
consists of a pseudofunctor

D\colon S\to\bf{ExpAct}
such that
p\circ D=d
.
Spelling this out, this amounts to specifying the following data:
For each mode
\mathsf{m}
, an SMCC
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{m}}
together with an exponential
action
\and_{\mathsf{m}}
of
\mathsf{m}
on
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{m}}
.
And whenever
\mathsf{m}\leq\mathsf{n}
, a morphism of exponential actions

(\varphi_{\mathsf{m},\mathsf{n}},F^{\mathsf{n}}_{\mathsf{m}},G^{\mathsf{n}}_{\mathsf{m}},\ell^{\mathsf{n}}_{\mathsf{m}})\colon(\mathsf{m},\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{m}},\and_{\mathsf{m}})\to(\mathsf{n},\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{n}},\and_{\mathsf{n}})

where
\varphi_{\mathsf{m},\mathsf{n}}
is the morphism of grade algebras specified
in Definition
3.1

along with a coherent system of natural isomorphisms

G^{\mathsf{m}_{3}}_{\mathsf{m}_{2}}\circ G^{\mathsf{m}_{2}}_{\mathsf{m}_{1}}\cong G^{\mathsf{m}_{3}}_{\mathsf{m}_{1}}

whenever
\mathsf{m}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq\mathsf{m}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\leq\mathsf{m}_{{\mathrm{3}}}

which are compatible with the lineators.

We write

u\colon I\to F^{\mathsf{m}}_{\mathsf{n}}(I)
and

m\colon F^{\mathsf{m}}_{\mathsf{n}}(A)\otimes F^{\mathsf{m}}_{\mathsf{n}}(B)\to F^{\mathsf{m}}_{\mathsf{n}}(A\otimes B)

for the strong monoidal structure on the functors
F^{\mathsf{m}}_{\mathsf{n}}
.
We denote internal hom-objects by
X\multimap Y
.
We write
\textit{ev}\colon(X\multimap Y)\otimes X\to Y
for the counit of the
of the adjunction
(-)\otimes X\dashv X\multimap(-)
.
Given
e\colon X\otimes Y\to Z
we write
\lambda e\colon X\to(Y\multimap Z)

for the transpose of
e
along this adjunction.

Example 4.13
.

Let
F\dashv G\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{L}
be a symmetric lax monoidal adjunction.
Suppose that
\mathsf{L}
acts on
\mathcal{L}
as described in
Example
4.4
,
and that
\mathcal{C}
admits an exponential action by a mode
\mathsf{m}=(R,R,\mathsf{true})

for some grade algebra
R
.
We promote this adjunction to a morphism of exponential actions with lineator

\varphi(n)\and G(A)=(\varphi(1)+\mathellipsis+\varphi(1))\and G(A)\xrightarrow{l}G(A)^{\otimes n}\xrightarrow{z}G(A^{\otimes n})

where the first arrow,
l
, is
c
(resp.
w
if
n=0
)
and the second arrow,
z
, is the monoidal structure on
G
.
Therefore, any model of

mGL

[
31
]

is a model of
Grass
instantiated
with the modes
\mathsf{L}<\mathsf{m}
.
When
\mathsf{m}=\mathsf{U}
,

\mathcal{C}
is cartesian closed, and
\mathsf{U}
acts on
\mathcal{C}
by
1\and(-)=\id
,
as described in Example
4.4
,
we recover the categorical situation of LNL
[
4
]
.

Definition 4.14
.

Recall the rules for well-formed types from Figure
1
.
We assign each type
\mathit{A}\in\mathsf{Type}(\mathsf{m})
an object of
\llbracket A\rrbracket\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{m}}

by induction on the proof of
\mathit{A}\in\mathsf{Type}(\mathsf{m})
.

\frac{}{\llbracket\mathbf{I}_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket=I\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{m}}}\quad\textsc{unit}\and\frac{\mathit{A}\in\mathsf{Type}(\mathsf{m})\and\mathit{B}\in\mathsf{Type}(\mathsf{m})}{\llbracket A\otimes B\rrbracket=\llbracket A\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{m}}}\quad\textsc{pair}\and\frac{\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}\and\mathit{q}\in R_{\mathsf{m}}\quad\quad\mathit{A}\in\mathsf{Type}(\mathsf{m})\and\mathit{B}\in\mathsf{Type}(\mathsf{n})}{\llbracket\mathit{A}^{\mathit{q}:\mathsf{m}}\multimap\mathit{B}\rrbracket=F^{\mathsf{m}}_{\mathsf{n}}(q\and\llbracket A\rrbracket)\multimap\llbracket B\rrbracket\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{n}}}\quad\textsc{fun}\and\frac{\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}\and\mathit{q}\in R_{\mathsf{m}}\and\mathit{A}\in\mathsf{Type}(\mathsf{m})}{\llbracket\operatorname{\downarrow}^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}}\mathit{A}\rrbracket=F^{\mathsf{m}}_{\mathsf{n}}(q\and\llbracket A\rrbracket)\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{n}}}\quad\textsc{drop}\and\frac{\mathsf{m}\leq\mathsf{n}\and\mathit{A}\in\mathsf{Type}(\mathsf{m})}{\llbracket\operatorname{\uparrow}_{\mathsf{m}\leq\mathsf{n}}\mathit{A}\rrbracket=G^{\mathsf{n}}_{\mathsf{m}}\llbracket A\rrbracket\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{n}}}\quad\textsc{drop}

We will often omit the brackets
\llbracket-\rrbracket
writing
A
instead of
\llbracket A\rrbracket
.

Definition 4.15
.

Let
\rho=\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{r_{\mathit{k}}}

and
\mathsf{M}=\mathsf{m}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathsf{m}_{\mathit{k}}

and
\Gamma=\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{x_{\mathit{k}}}:\mathit{A_{\mathit{k}}}
,
and
\mathsf{M}\geq\mathsf{m}
.
Define

\llbracket\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\odot\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathsf{m}}:=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}\llbracket\operatorname{\downarrow}^{\mathit{r_{\mathit{i}}}}_{\mathsf{m}\leq\mathsf{m}_{\mathit{i}}}\mathit{A_{\mathit{i}}}\rrbracket=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}F^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathit{i}}}_{\mathsf{m}}(r_{i}\and A_{i})

Note that there is a canonical isomorphism

F^{\mathsf{m}}_{\mathsf{n}}(\llbracket\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\odot\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathsf{m}})\cong\llbracket\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\odot\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathsf{n}}

whenever
\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}
.

Definition 4.16
.

In the situation of Definition
4.15
, let
\mathit{q}\in R_{\mathsf{m}}

and let
t\colon\llbracket\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\odot\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathsf{m}}\to A
be a morphism.
Define
q\cdot t\colon\llbracket\mathit{q}\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\odot\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathsf{m}}\to q\and A
as

\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}F^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathit{i}}}_{\mathsf{m}}(q\cdot r_{i})\and A_{i}\xrightarrow{\tau\circ\bigotimes_{i}(\mu\circ F^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathit{i}}}_{\mathsf{m}}\delta)}q\and\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}F^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathit{i}}}_{\mathsf{m}}(r_{i}\and A_{i})\xrightarrow{q\and t}q\and A

for
k>0
.
When
k=0
, define

q\cdot t=(q\and t)\circ\iota\colon I_{\mathsf{m}}\to q\and I_{\mathsf{m}}\to q\and A
.

Name

Type

Isomorphism?

\delta

(rq)\and X\to r\and(q\and X)

no

\epsilon

1\and X\to X

no

\tau

(r\and X)\otimes(r\and Y)\to r\and(X\otimes Y)

yes

yes

yes

yes

no

5
Conclusion, related and future work

We defined a graded logic which allows grades
from multiple modes to exist in a single system.
Furthermore, we have generalized the graded weakening and contraction rules
to obtain more fine-grained control in the graded setting.
This allowed us to recover non-graded substructural logics
in an entirely graded setting.
We gave an account of the categorical semantics of our logic based on graded
comonads.
Our logic consists of multiple graded systems connected by morphisms of modes,
and this design is mirrored in the categorical semantics.
We introduced a novel notion of morphisms of graded comonads,
and showed how it connects to the existing literature.
We saw that categorical models of substructural logics
provide categorical models for the modes which recover such logics
at the syntactic level.

Combined systems

Systems which combine logics permitting different sets of structural rules
originate in Benton’s Linear-non-Linear Logic (LNL)
[
4
]
,
a system which combines linear and intuitionistic logics.
A similar result was achieved for graded and linear logics in
mGL

of Vollmer et al.
[
31
]
.
In the non-graded world, LNL was further generalized by Pruiksma et al.
into Adjoint Logic
[
27
]
,
which allows an arbitrary collection of logics with different structural rules
(relevant, affine, linear, intuitionistic) to exist in one system.
The present work initially started out as a “graded Adjoint Logic,”
and thus bears many similarities to Adjoint Logic.
For example, our mode system and modal operators

\operatorname{\uparrow}_{\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}}\mathit{A}
and
\operatorname{\downarrow}^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathsf{n}\leq\mathsf{m}}\mathit{A}

are direct adaptations of the mode system and modal operators presented
in Adjoint Logic.
All of the systems mentioned here have one observation in common:
Terms from logics with fewer structural rules may depend on variables permitting
more structural rules, but not vice-versa.
This invariant is also present in our work,
and we leverage it to ensure that reuse of more structured variables
is well-behaved in less structured modes.

Heterogenously graded systems

Systems where grades can be drawn from more than one grade algebra
have been studied for subsets of Java by
Bianchini et al.
[
5
,
6
]

as well as Giannini and Duso
[
14
]
.
We refer to these works collectively as
Graded Java
.
Modes in
Grass
correspond to
kinds
of coeffects in Graded Java.
Both modes and kinds are arranged in a preorder,
and both systems use morphisms of grade algebras to combine (i.e. add or multiply)
grades from different algebras.

We highlight some differences:
In Graded Java, the order on kinds is required to admit joins,
and when grades from different kinds
k_{1},k_{2}
are combined,
the result belongs to kind
k_{1}\lor k_{2}
.
This means that the kind of coeffect a variable is annotated with might change
during the typing derivation, which can lead to a loss of information
since morphisms of grade algebras need not be injective.
In contrast to this,

Grass
’s typing rules ensure that additions only happen between grades from
the same mode,
and multiplications only happen when the modes are comparable in the preorder,
thus avoiding the need for joins.
Furthermore each variable in
Grass
belongs to a unique mode,
and its grades are always guaranteed to be drawn from that mode.
Hence there is no risk of the loss of information mentioned above.
We leave it to future work to investigate if/how our approach can be implemented
in a system like Graded Java.

Categorical semantics of graded modal types

Many existing graded type systems have been given categorical semantics

[
8
,
12
,
13
,
25
,
26
]
.
These semantics were all somewhat similar,
employing a notion of
graded linear exponential comonads

(though terminology varied).
Later, Katsumata
[
16
]
gave a comprehensive account
of graded linear exponential comonads.
Katsumata’s work also serves as the basis for the exponential actions defined in
the present text.
Beyond the changes that were necessary to account for our modified weakening
and contraction rules,
there is one other difference between our presentation and that of Katsumata:
We require that
r\and(-)
be a strong monoidal functor,
while the categorical semantics in the work cited here all require this functor only to
be lax monoidal.
We attribute this difference to the fact that strong monoidality of
r\and(-)

is required for the interpretation of the tensor product type,
which was not included in the above works.
Cateogrical semantics were given to
mGL

by Vollmer et al.
[
31
]
.
This type system does have a tensor product,
and they require the functors
r\and(-)

to be strict monoidal, i.e. the morphism

(r\and A)\otimes(r\and B)\to r\and(A\otimes B)
must be an identity.
We have shown here that the strictness is not necessary for the interpretation of
the graded tensor product.
Finally, Atkey
[
2
]
has given categorical semantics
to a dependently typed graded system using
Quantitative Categories with Families
,
an approach which is not based on graded comonads.

Future work

One future direction is to use the lessons learned here about the categorical semantics
of graded types towards a categorical semantics of graded
dependent
types
which is based on graded comonads.
One problem in this area is that uses of terms inside of types are considered
computationally irrelevant, and therefore exempt from grading.
In other words, types are treated as intuitionistic, but terms must still be graded.
The categorical semantics presented here are able to capture
graded and intuitionistic variables existing in the same system,
by instantiating
Grass
with any one mode for the graded system, together
with the terminal mode to recover intuitionistic logic.
We hope to use this as a starting point for the categorical semantics
of graded dependent types.

Appendix A
Specification of the single mode system

This section contains the specification for the single mode specialization of
Grass
.
The syntax for types and terms, and the corresponding typing rules are

\begin{array}[]{rrl}\textit{(Types)}&A,B,C,T::=&\mathbf{I}\mid\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B}\mid\mathit{A}\oplus\mathit{B}\mid\mathit{A}^{\mathit{q}}\multimap\mathit{B}\mid\square_{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}\\
\textit{(Terms)}&e,f,t::=&\mathit{x}\mid\star\mid\mathbin{\mathsf{let}}_{@\mathit{q}}\star=\mathit{t}\mathbin{\mathsf{in}}\mathit{e}\mid(\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\mid\mathbin{\mathsf{let}}_{@\mathit{q}}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\mathit{t}\mathbin{\mathsf{in}}\mathit{e}\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}\,\mathit{t}\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}\,\mathit{t}\\
&&\mid\mathsf{case}_{\mathit{q}}(\mathit{t};\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\mid\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{t}\mid\mathit{f}\,\mathit{t}\mid\square_{\mathit{q}}\mathit{t}\mid\mathbin{\mathsf{let}}_{@\mathit{q}}\square\,\mathit{x}=\mathit{t}\mathbin{\mathsf{in}}\mathit{e}\end{array}

\frac{}{1\odot\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{S.Var}}{},}\and\frac{\mathsf{Weak}(\mathsf{m})\quad\rho\odot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{A}}{\rho,0\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{B}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{S.Weak}}{},}\and\frac{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\in\mathsf{Cont}(\mathsf{m})\quad\rho,\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A},\mathit{y}:\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{B}}{\rho,\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{z}:\mathit{A}\vdash[\mathit{z}/\mathit{x},\mathit{z}/\mathit{y}]\mathit{e}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{S.Cont}}{},}\and\frac{\rho\leq\sigma\quad\rho\odot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{A}}{\sigma\odot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{S.Sub}}{},}\and\frac{}{\emptyset\odot\emptyset\vdash\star:\mathbf{I}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{S.Unit.i}}{},}\and\frac{\sigma\odot\Delta\vdash\mathit{t}:\mathbf{I}\quad\rho\odot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{A}}{\rho,\mathit{q}\cdot\sigma\odot\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\mathbin{\mathsf{let}}_{@\mathit{q}}\star=\mathit{t}\mathbin{\mathsf{in}}\mathit{e}:\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{S.Unit.e}}{},}\and\frac{\rho\odot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\quad\sigma\odot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\rho,\sigma\odot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash(\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\otimes\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{S.Pair.i}}{},}\and\frac{\sigma\odot\Delta\vdash\mathit{t}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\otimes\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\rho,\mathit{q},\mathit{q}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{B}}{\rho,\mathit{q}\cdot\sigma\odot\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\mathbin{\mathsf{let}}_{@\mathit{q}}(\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}})=\mathit{t}\mathbin{\mathsf{in}}\mathit{e}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{S.Pair.e}}{},}\and\frac{\rho\odot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{A}}{\rho\odot\Gamma\vdash\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}\,\mathit{e}:\mathit{A}\oplus\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{S.Sum.il}}{},}\and\frac{\rho\odot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{B}}{\rho\odot\Gamma\vdash\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}\,\mathit{e}:\mathit{A}\oplus\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{S.Sum.ir}}{},}\and\frac{\sigma\odot\Delta\vdash\mathit{t}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\oplus\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\rho,\mathit{q}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\vdash\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{B}\quad\rho,\mathit{q}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}}{\rho,\mathit{q}\cdot\sigma\odot\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\mathsf{case}_{\mathit{q}}(\mathit{t};\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{S.Sum.e}}{},}\and\frac{\rho,\mathit{q}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{t}:\mathit{B}}{\rho\odot\Gamma\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}^{\mathit{q}}\multimap\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{S.Fun.i}}{},}\and\frac{\rho\odot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}^{\mathit{q}}\multimap\mathit{B}\quad\sigma\odot\Delta\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{A}}{\rho,\mathit{q}\cdot\sigma\odot\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\mathit{t}\,\mathit{e}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{S.Fun.e}}{},}\and\frac{\rho\odot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}}{\mathit{q}\cdot\rho\odot\Gamma\vdash\square_{\mathit{q}}\mathit{t}:\square_{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{S.Box.i}}{},}\and\frac{\rho,\mathit{q}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{t}:\mathit{B}\quad\sigma\odot\Delt Appendix B
Syntactic properties of
Grass

B.1
Substitution theorem

Theorem B.1
.

If
\rho,\mathit{r}\mid\mathsf{M},\mathsf{m}\odot\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash_{\mathsf{l}}\mathit{t}\colon\mathit{B}

and
\sigma\mid\mathsf{N}\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}\mathit{e}\colon\mathit{A}
,
then
\rho,\mathit{r}\sigma\mid\mathsf{M},\mathsf{N}\odot\Gamma,\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{l}}[\mathit{e}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{t}\colon\mathit{B}
.

Due to the presence of an exlicit contraction rule,
we cannot prove this theorem directly.
Instead we must strengthen the inductive hypothesis to make the structural
induction work out.

Theorem B.2

(Simultaneous Subsitution)
.

Suppose that
\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\odot\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}\mathit{t}\colon\mathit{T}

with
\rho=\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{r_{\mathit{k}}}
,

\mathsf{M}=\mathsf{m}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathsf{m}_{\mathit{k}}
and

\Gamma=\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{x_{\mathit{k}}}:\mathit{A_{\mathit{k}}}
.
Suppose that for each
i\in\{1,\mathellipsis,k\}
we have a term

\sigma_{\mathit{i}}\mid\mathsf{N}_{\mathit{i}}\odot\Delta_{\mathit{i}}\vdash_{\mathsf{m}_{\mathit{i}}}\mathit{e_{\mathit{i}}}\colon\mathit{A_{\mathit{i}}}
.
Then

\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sigma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{r_{\mathit{k}}}\sigma_{\mathit{k}}\mid\mathsf{N}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathsf{N}_{\mathit{k}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\Delta_{\mathit{k}}\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}[\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\,..\,,\mathit{e_{\mathit{k}}}/\mathit{x_{\mathit{k}}}]\mathit{t}\colon\mathit{T}

We may abbreviate this term as
[\mathit{e_{\mathit{i}}}/\mathit{x_{\mathit{i}}}]\mathit{t}
.

Proof B.3
.

By induction on the derivation of
\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\odot\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}\mathit{t}\colon\mathit{T}
.

{case}

[
Var
]
In this case
k=1
and
T=A_{1}
and the derivation is

\frac{}{1\mid\mathsf{m}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\colon\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}.

We must show that
\sigma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mid\mathsf{N}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{m}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\colon\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
which is an
assumption.

{case}

[
Weak
]
In this case the derivation is

\frac{\Weak(\mathsf{m}_{k+1})\and\mathsf{m}\leq\mathsf{m}_{k+1}\and A_{k+1}\in\mathsf{Type}(\mathsf{m}_{k+1})\and\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\odot\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}\mathit{t}\colon\mathit{T}}{\rho,0\mid\mathsf{M},\mathsf{m}_{k+1}\and\Gamma,x_{k+1}:A_{k+1}\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}t:T}

The inductive hypothesis is

r_{1}\cdot\sigma_{1},\mathellipsis,r_{k}\cdot\sigma_{k}\mid\mathsf{N}_{1},\mathellipsis,\mathsf{N}_{k}\and\Delta_{1},\mathellipsis,\Delta_{k}\vdash[\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\,..\,,\mathit{e_{\mathit{k}}}/\mathit{x_{\mathit{k}}}]\mathit{t}:T

and we need to show that

r_{1}\cdot\sigma_{1},\mathellipsis,r_{k}\cdot\sigma_{k},0\cdot\sigma_{k+1}\mid\mathsf{N}_{1},\mathellipsis,\mathsf{N}_{k},\mathsf{N}_{k+1}\and\Delta_{1},\mathellipsis,\Delta_{k},\Delta_{k+1}\vdash[e_{1}/x_{1},..,e_{k}/x_{k},e_{k+1}/x_{k+1}]t:T.

From

\rho_{k+1}\mid\mathsf{N}_{k+1}\and\Delta_{k+1}\vdash_{\mathsf{m}_{k+1}}e_{k+1}:A_{k+1}

it follows that
\mathsf{N}\geq\mathsf{m}_{k+1}
.
Together with
\Weak\mathsf{m}_{k+1}
,
this implies that
\Weak(\mathsf{n})
holds for each
\mathsf{n}
in
\mathsf{N}_{k+1}
.
Hence we may apply a sequence of weakenings to to weaken by each type
occuring in
\Delta_{k+1}
to obtain

r_{1}\cdot\sigma_{1},\mathellipsis,r_{k}\cdot\sigma_{k},0\cdot\sigma_{k+1}\mid\mathsf{N}_{1},\mathellipsis,\mathsf{N}_{k},\mathsf{N}_{k+1}\and\Delta_{1},\mathellipsis,\Delta_{k},\Delta_{k+1}\vdash[e_{1}/x_{1},..,e_{k}/x_{k}]t:T.

which is the desired judgment since
x_{k+1}
does not occur freely in
t
.

{case}

[
Sub
]
In this case we have
\rho^{\prime}=r_{1}^{\prime},\mathellipsis,r_{k}^{\prime}

and
r_{i}^{\prime}\leq r_{i}
for each
i
and the derivation is

\frac{\rho^{\prime}\mid\mathsf{M}\and\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}t:T}{\rho\mid\mathsf{M}\and\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}t:T}

By monotonicity of multiplication, we have

\mathit{r^{\prime}_{\mathit{i}}}\sigma_{\mathit{i}}\leq\mathit{r_{\mathit{i}}}\sigma_{\mathit{i}}
for each
i

and hence
\mathit{r^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sigma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{r^{\prime}_{\mathit{k}}}\sigma_{\mathit{k}}\leq\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sigma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{r_{\mathit{k}}}\sigma_{\mathit{k}}
.
The following application of the
Sub
rule

\frac{\mathit{r^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sigma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{r^{\prime}_{\mathit{k}}}\sigma_{\mathit{k}}\mid\mathsf{N}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathsf{N}_{\mathit{k}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\Delta_{\mathit{k}}\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}[\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\,..\,,\mathit{e_{\mathit{k}}}/\mathit{x_{\mathit{k}}}]\mathit{t}\colon\mathit{T}\and\mathit{r^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sigma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{r^{\prime}_{\mathit{k}}}\sigma_{\mathit{k}}\leq\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sigma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{r_{\mathit{k}}}\sigma_{\mathit{k}}}{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sigma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{r_{\mathit{k}}}\sigma_{\mathit{k}}\mid\mathsf{N}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathsf{N}_{\mathit{k}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\Delta_{\mathit{k}}\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}[\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\,..\,,\mathit{e_{\mathit{k}}}/\mathit{x_{\mathit{k}}}]\mathit{t}\colon\mathit{T}}

yields the desired result.

{case}

[
Cont
]
The derivation has the shape

\frac{\rho,r_{k},r_{k+1}\mid\mathsf{M},\mathsf{m}_{k},\mathsf{m}_{k}\and\Gamma,x_{k}:A_{k},x_{k+1}:A_{k}\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}t:T\and r_{k},r_{k+1}\in\Cont\mathsf{m}}{\rho,r_{k}+r_{k+1}\mid\mathsf{M},\mathsf{m}_{k}\and\Gamma,z:A_{k}\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}[z/x_{k},z/x_{k+1}]t:T}

We apply the inductive hypothesis with
e_{k+1}=e_{k}
to obtain

r_{1}\cdot\sigma_{1},\mathellipsis,r_{k}\cdot\sigma_{k},r_{k+1}\cdot\sigma_{k}\mid\mathsf{N}_{1},\mathellipsis,\mathsf{N}_{k},\mathsf{N}_{k}\and\Delta_{1},\mathellipsis,\Delta_{k},\Delta_{k}\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}[e_{1}/x_{1},\mathellipsis,e_{k}/x_{k},e_{k}/x_{k+1}]t:T

Since
\Cont\mathsf{m}
is an ideal, each entry in
r_{k}\cdot\sigma_{k}

and
r_{k+1}\cdot\sigma_{k}
is in
\Cont\mathsf{m}
.
A sequence of exchanges followed by a sequence of contractions yields

r_{1}\cdot\sigma_{1},\mathellipsis,(r_{k}+r_{k+1})\cdot\sigma_{k}\mid\mathsf{N}_{1},\mathellipsis,\mathsf{N}_{k}\and\Delta_{1},\mathellipsis,\Delta_{k}\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}[e_{1}/x_{1},\mathellipsis,e_{k}/x_{k},e_{k}/x_{k+1}]t:T.

The term in the desired result

[e_{1}/x_{1},\mathellipsis,e_{k-1}/x_{k-1},e_{k}/z][z/x_{k},z/x_{k+1}]t

which is equal to the one above.

{case}

[
Unit.i
]
There is nothing to prove in this case.

(

\displaystyle r_{1}\sigma_{1},\mathellipsis,r_{k}\sigma_{k},qr_{1}^{\prime}\cdot\sigma_{1}^{\prime},\mathellipsis,qr_{k}\cdot\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime}\mid\mathsf{N}_{1},\mathellipsis,\mathsf{N}_{k},\mathsf{N}_{1}^{\prime},\mathellipsis,\mathsf{N}_{\ell}^{\prime}

\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sigma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{r_{\mathit{k}}}\sigma_{\mathit{k}}\mid\mathsf{N}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathsf{N}_{\mathit{k}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\Delta_{\mathit{k}}\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}[\mathit{e_{\mathit{i}}}/\mathit{x_{\mathit{i}}}]\mathit{t}\colon\mathit{T}\and\mathit{r^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{r^{\prime}_{\mathit{\ell}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{\mathit{\ell}}\mid\mathsf{N}^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathsf{N}^{\prime}_{\mathit{\ell}}\odot\Delta^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\Delta^{\prime}_{\mathit{\ell}}\vdash_{\mathsf{m}}[\mathit{e^{\prime}_{\mathit{i}}}/\mathit{x^{\prime}_{\mathit{i}}}]\mathit{t^{\prime}}\colon\mathbf{I}_{\mathsf{m}}

applying the rule
Unit.e
to these judgments,
we obtain the desired result, since

\mathit{q}(\mathit{r^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{r^{\prime}_{\mathit{\ell}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{\mathit{\ell}})=\mathit{q}\mathit{r^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathellipsis,\mathit{q}\mathit{r^{\prime}_{\mathit{\ell}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{\mathit{\ell}}
